# 2604.00638v1_Explicit_minimal_generating_sets_of_a_family_of_prime_ideals_with_unbounded_mini

## Page 1


EXPLICIT MINIMAL GENERATING SETS OF A FAMILY OF PRIME
IDEALS WITH UNBOUNDED MINIMAL NUMBER OF GENERATORS IN A
THREE-DIMENSIONAL POWER SERIES RING
LAURA GONZ´ALEZ AND FRANCESC PLANAS-VILANOVA
Abstract. We display a new family of prime ideals with unbounded minimal number of genera-
tors in a three-dimensional power series ring over a field of characteristic zero. These primes are
obtained as the kernel of a quasi-monomial algebra homomorphism. Up to constant coefficients,
determined by some specific linear systems with binomial entries, we describe their minimal gen-
erating polynomial sets. The advantage of our family with respect to some previous work is, on
the one hand, the explicit description of the generating sets and, on the other hand, the simplicity
of the exponents of the aforementioned quasi-monomial homomorphism. We also provide a code
in Python which states and solves the linear systems that lead to a complete description of the
minimal generating sets with a “Gr¨obner-free” approach.

1. Introduction
Let R = k[[x, y, z]] be the power series ring in the variables x, y, z over a field k of characteristic
zero. In 1974, T.T. Moh [15] inspired by the work of S.S. Abhyankar on Macaulay’s primes [1] and
by the research of J. Herzog [10] (see also [11, p. 137]) on three-dimensional affine monomial curves,
considered the following family of prime ideals in R: let n ≥1 be an odd integer, m = (n + 1)/2
and λ an integer greater than n(n + 1)m, with gcd(λ, m) = 1; let ρn : k[[x, y, z]] →k[[t]] be the
k-algebra “quasi-monomial” homomorphism defined by ρn(x) = tnm(1 + tλ), ρn(y) = t(n+1)m and
ρn(z) = t(n+2)m and let Qn = ker(ρn). Then he showed that the minimal number of generators
of Qn is µ(Qn) ≥n + 1. In his final remarks in [15], Moh writes that, when looking for a three-
dimensional irreducible affine curve which is not set theoretically a complete intersection, if there is
any, it would seem reasonable to start with examples of prime ideals which are “highly non-complete
intersection”.
2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. 13E15, 13H05, 13J05, 13P10, 14H50.
Key words and phrases. Power series ring, prime ideal, minimal generating set, monomial curve.
Both authors are partially supported by Grant PID2023-146936NB-I00 financed by the Spanish State Agency
MCIN/AEI/10.13039/501100011033/ FEDER, UE and AGAUR project 2021 SGR 00603.

## Page 2


2
L. GONZ´ALEZ AND F. PLANAS-VILANOVA
Shortly after, in [16], Moh himself shows that, in fact, µ(Qn) = n + 1. Observe that, for each n,
Qn is a perfect prime ideal of height 2, hence, by the Hilbert-Burch Theorem (see, e.g., [3, § 1.4]),
Qn is the ideal of the n × n minors of a n × (n + 1) matrix with entries in R. The structure of this
matrix for the leading forms of the generators of Qn is given in [16]. In addition, in both papers,
[15], [16], Moh states that as a consequence of the existence of Qn in R, one can deduce the same
result in the polynomial ring A = k[x, y, z].
J. Sally in her book [19, p. 58 and ff.] gives an excellent overview on the work of Moh and
suggests that the limitation to characteristic zero can be avoided a posteriori. Moh in [16] quotes
the work of J. Sally as an “interesting argument to drop the restriction on the characteristic of the
ground field k”. However, in [7], the authors of the present paper show that the minimal number of
generators of Qn does depend on the characteristic of the ground field and, in fact, may decrease.
In 1980, in an unpublished work [13], J. Maurer affirms that Moh finds inductively the power
series which generate Qn, but that it is not clear whether they are or not polynomials. This is
a relevant point, for instance, in order to deduce the existence of such primes in the polynomial
ring A = k[x, y, z]. Maurer slightly modifies the prime ideals of Moh by considering the k-algebra
homomorphism ρn : k[[x, y, z]] →k[[t]] defined by ρn(x) = tan, ρn(y) = tbn and ρn(z) = tcn(1 + t),
where (an, bn, cn) = n+1
2 (n, n + 1, n + 2) if n is odd, and (an, bn, cn) = n
2 (n + 1, n + 2, n + 3) if n
is even. Then he states that ker(ρn) is generated by the n + 1 polynomials obtained as the n × n
minors of an explicit, up to constants to be found, n × (n + 1) matrix with entries in A = k[x, y, z].
We are convinced that the extraordinary work of Maurer, as well as those of Moh, will give birth
to new ideas and results. We would like to thank Anna Oneto for providing us with a copy of the
work of Maurer.
Perhaps in this direction, recently, Mehta, Saha and Sengupta [14] give an iterative method for
finding a set of polynomials that minimally generates the prime ideals Qn of Moh, though, the
authors say, “it is not very clear to us whether they are the same as those conceived in Moh’s work
or whether there is any underlying determinantal structure”.
As said by Moh, obtaining families of prime ideals in R = k[[x, y, z]] or A = k[x, y, z] which
need an arbitrarily large number of generators may lead to help to understand the open problem of
whether or not a three-dimensional affine curve is set-theoretically a complete intersection. In the
same vein, it may help to face the open problem stated by Sally in [19, Remark p. 53] on whether a
uniform bound on the number of generators of prime ideals implies that the ring has dimension at
most two. Or to undertake the apparently more simple conjecture stated by Shimoda on whether
a noetherian local ring such that all its prime ideals different from the maximal ideal are complete
intersection, has dimension at most two (see, e.g., [9]).
Following the suggestion of Moh and some of his ideas, we consider here the following family
of prime ideals Pn. Let n be an integer, n ≥3, a = (n −1)n/2 and q = 2⌊(n + 1)/2⌋−1. Let
ρn : k[[x, y, z]] →k[[t]] be the k-algebra “quasi-monomial” homomorphism defined by ρn(x) =
ta(1 + tq), ρn(y) = ta+1, ρn(z) = ta+2. We prove that the prime ideal Pn = ker(ρn) is minimally
generated by an explicitly given, up to constants, set of n polynomials f1, . . . , fn (see the next
section for the definition of the fi and the statement of the main result). Moreover, the constant
coefficients of each fi are just found by solving at most four determined consistent linear systems
of size at most n. We provide the reader with a code in Python to help in finding f1, . . . , fn (see
Remark 8.2). We emphasize that there is no need to use a Gr¨obner basis approach to calculate
the polynomials f1, . . . , fn, which clearly shortens the time to obtain the minimal generating sets
of each Pn, especially for higher integers n.
The advantage of our primes with respect to those given by Moh resides in that we can explicitly
find their minimal generating sets. With respect to Maurer’s examples we explicit the linear systems
that have to be solved to find the coefficients of the generators. Another advantage with respect
to both the primes of Moh and Maurer is in the exponents of the homomorphism ρn. Namely, the
exponents nm, (n+1)m and (n+2)m in Moh’s examples and the exponents (an, bn, cn) in Maurer’s


## Page 3


EXPLICIT MINIMAL GENERATING SETS OF PRIME IDEALS
3
examples are not coprime, whereas our exponents (a, a+1, a+2) are coprime. In particular, the set
{a, a + 1, a + 2} generates a numerical semigroup, whose structure will be crucial to our purposes.
On the other hand, having coprime exponents, we believe, may be crucial in extending these results
to a noetherian, non necessarily regular, local ring (see, e.g., the argument in [17, page 4]). Here
we thank the software system Singular [4] for serving as an excellent source of inspiration. In
particular, we would like to highlight the merit of the previous work of Moh and Maurer, when
no such software was available. Since Pn is minimally generated by n polynomials f1, . . . , fn, for
each n ≥3, we can deduce the existence of prime ideals in k[x, y, z] and k[x, y, z](x,y,z) minimally
generated by at least n elements (see Corollary 7.5). As said before, and in our opinion, this aspect
seems insufficiently justified in Moh’s papers. With respect to the question of Sally, we deduce that
a regular local ring containing a field of characteristic zero has, for each integer n ≥3, a prime
ideal minimally generated by n elements (see Corollary 7.6).
Some questions should be addressed in the future.
The first question would be to find the
syzygy Hilbert-Burch matrix to the polynomials f1, . . . , fn. The second problem is related to the
characteristic of the ground field: we hope that our family contains subfamilies with unbounded
minimal number of generators in characteristic other than zero. Some work has been done in this
direction in characteristic 3. An affirmative answer would lead to the solution of the question of
Sally in equicharacteristic regular local rings. Thirdly, we would like to prove (or disprove) that
our primes are set-theoretically a complete intersection.
The proof of our main result is based, roughly speaking, in three pillars.
The first pillar is
a stratification of the elements of the numerical semigroup S = ⟨a, a + 1, a + 2⟩which follows
from a previous work in [5]. The second pillar is [7, Theorem 3.2], an extension of Moh’s [15,
Theorem 4.3], in which subsets of linearly independent elements of some vector spaces Vr, r ∈S,
related to the leading forms of the elements of Pn, determine parts of a minimal generating set.
Once the candidates f1, . . . , fn of a minimal generating set of Pn are found so that (f1, . . . , fn) ⊆Pn,
the third pillar is the calculation of the length of the ideals (f1, . . . , fn, z) and (Pn, z) to deduce the
equality (f1, . . . , fn) = Pn.
An overview of the whole paper is given in the next section.
2. Statement of the main result and overview of the paper
In this section we state the main result while, at the same time, we introduce the indispensable
notations to follow the paper. Subsequently, we give a general overview of the whole article.
Notation 2.1. Throughout, n is an integer, n ≥3, and a = (n −1)n/2, so that a is an integer,
a ≥3. Let q = 2⌊(n + 1)/2⌋−1, where ⌊λ⌋stands for the floor function of λ, the largest integer
less than or equal to λ ∈Q. Let s0 = a(n −1) + 2. This integer, we will see, will play an important
role.
Let k be a field of characteristic zero. Let k[[x, y, z]] and k[[t]] be the power series rings in three
variables x, y, z and in one variable t, respectively, with coefficients in k.
Let S = ⟨a, a + 1, a + 2⟩be the numerical semigroup generated by a, a + 1, a + 2. Its Frobenius
number, i.e., the largest integer that does not belong to S, is Frob(S) = ⌊a/2⌋a −1. For r ∈N, the
set of natural numbers, including zero, let
F(r, S) = {α = (α1, α2, α3) ∈N3 | α1a + α2(a + 1) + α3(a + 2) = r}
be the set of factorizations of r. If r ̸∈S, then F(r, S) = ∅. Let mα = xα1yα1zα3 denote the
monomial in x = x, y, z with multi-index exponent α = (α1, α2, α3) ∈N3.
The degree of mα
is defined as deg(mα) = |α| = α1 + α2 + α3. Any power series of k[[x]] can be written as f =
P
α∈N3 λαmα, with λα ∈k. The support of f, f ̸= 0, is Supp(f) = {α ∈N3 | λα ̸= 0}. The
order of f, f ̸= 0, is ord(f) = min{|α| ∈N | α ∈Supp(f)}. The leading form of f, f ̸= 0, is
LF(f) = P
|α|=ord(f) λαmα; f is said to be homogeneous if f = LF(f).

The purpose of the present work is to prove the following result.
Main Theorem. Let n be an integer, n ≥3. Let a = (n −1)n/2 and q = 2⌊(n + 1)/2⌋−1.
Let ρ : k[[x, y, z]] →k[[t]] be the k-algebra homomorphism with ρ(x) = ta(1 + tq), ρ(y) = ta+1,
ρ(z) = ta+2. Then the prime ideal ker(ρ) is minimally generated by the n polynomials f1, . . . , fn.
Their σ-leading forms satisfy fσ
1 = g1, . . . , fσ
n = gn. The coefficients of each tail fτ
i can be found
by solving at most four determined consistent linear systems of equations of size at most n.
Now, we sketch the proof. To do this, we require more notations.
Notation 2.4. (See [15] and [7, Remark 2.3 and Lemma 2.4].) Let r ∈N. Set
Wr = {g ∈k[[x]] | g ̸= 0, g = gσ, ordσ(g) = r} ∪{0}.
Clearly, Wr is the k-vector space spanned by the monomials mα, with α ∈F(r, S). Set δr = dim Wr.
If r ∈S, then Wr ̸= 0 and δr ≥1. If r = 0, then W0 = ⟨1⟩. If r ̸∈S, we just define Wr = {0}.
From now on, fix r ∈S, r ̸= 0. Set
Vr = {g ∈Wr | g ̸= 0, there exists f ∈ker(ρ), f ̸= 0, such that g = fσ} ∪{0}.
Then Vr is a k-vector subspace of Wr. Moreover, if g ∈Wr, g ̸= 0, then g ∈Vr if, and only if,
g ∈ker(ρ) or there exists h ∈k[[x]], h ̸= 0, such that ordσ(h) > r and such that f = g + h ∈ker(ρ).
In such a case, g = fσ and h = fτ.
The proof of the main theorem is based on [7, Theorem 3.2], an extended version of [15, Theo-
rem 4.3] (see also line 3 of the proof of the main result in [16, p. 310]). Below, and for the sake of
completeness and easy reference, we write an ad-hoc version, just called Extending to Basis Method,
sufficient to our purposes. Given a subset B of k[[x]], B ̸= ∅, {0}, let Bσ = {fσ | f ∈B, f ̸= 0}
denote the set of its σ-leading forms. If B = ∅or B = {0}, then we just define Bσ = ∅.

In Section 6 we find the coefficients of the polynomials f1, . . . , fn defined in Definition 2.3 so that
fi ∈ker(ρ) and fσ
i = gi. In particular, Vr = ker(ρr), for all r ∈[s0, s0 + n) and
s0 = min{ordσ(f) | f ∈ker(ρ), f ̸= 0}.
As a consequence, and using the Extending to Basis Method, we get the fourth step, “a part of a
minimal generating set of the kernel of ρ”:
Theorem 6.11. The polynomials f1, . . . , fn are part of a minimal generating set of ker(ρ).
In Section 7, we consider I = (f1, . . . , fn)R the ideal generated by the polynomials f1, . . . , fn.
We prove that the ideals (I, z) and (ker(ρ), z) have equal (finite) length. By the Nakayama Lemma
we obtain the last and fifth step, “a minimal generating set of the kernel of ρ”:
Theorem 7.4. The polynomials f1, . . . , fn define a minimal generating set of ker(ρ).
As a corollary, we deduce, for each n ≥3, the existence of prime ideals in k[x, y, z] and
k[x, y, z](x,y,z) minimally generated by n elements (see Corollary 7.5). We also deduce that the
Sally question has an affirmative answer for regular local rings containing a field of characteristic
zero (see Corollary 7.6).
We finish the paper with the case n = 6, as an example, and by giving a code to calculate the
polynomials f1, . . . , fn.

Lemma 3.1. Let r ∈S, r ̸= 0, and let α ∈F(r, S) and mα = xα1yα2zα3. The following hold.
(1) ρ(mα) = tr(1 + tq)α1 = bα1,0tr + bα1,1tr+q + · · · + bα1,α1tr+α1q, where bi,j =

(2) Supp(ρ(mα)) = {r + kq | k ∈[0, α1]}. In particular, ord(ρ(mα)) = r.
(3) If dim Wr = 1, then Vr = 0.
(4) Let g = P
α∈F(r,S) λαmα ∈Wr, g ̸= 0. If g ∈Vr, then P
α∈F(r,S) λα = 0.
(5) If Wr = ⟨mα, mβ⟩, with α, β ∈F(r, S), α ̸= β, then Vr = 0 or Vr = ⟨mα −mβ⟩.
Proof. Item (1) follows from the definition of ρ and the hypothesis α ∈F(r, S). Item (2) follows
directly from (1) and the fact that bα1,j ̸= 0, for all j = 0, . . . , α1.
Suppose that Wr = ⟨mα⟩, where α ∈F(r, S). Suppose that there exists g ∈Vr, g ̸= 0. By
definition, g ∈Wr, so g = λmα, for some λ ̸= 0. By Item (2), ord(ρ(g)) = r, thus, in particular,
g ̸∈ker(ρ). Since g ∈Vr and g ̸∈ker(ρ), by definition, there exists h ∈k[[x, y, z]], h ̸= 0, such
that ordσ(h) = s > r and ρ(g) + ρ(h) = 0. Since ρ(g) is not zero, ρ(h) cannot be zero. Applying
Item (1) to each monomial of h, one deduces that ord(ρ(h)) ≥s > r, which is a contradiction with
ρ(g) + ρ(h) = 0. Therefore, Vr must be zero, which proves (3).

Example 3.10. Suppose that n = 3, so a = (n −1)n/2 = 3, q = 2⌊(n + 1)/2⌋−1 = 3, s0 =
a(n −1) + 2 = 8, La = (⌊a/2⌋+ 2)a = 9, S = ⟨3, 4, 5⟩and ρ(x) = t3(1 + t3), ρ(y) = t4, ρ(z) = t5.
Since F(3, S) = {(1, 0, 0)}, F(4, S) = {(0, 1, 0)}, F(5, S) = {(0, 0, 1)}, F(6, S) = {(2, 0, 0)} and
F(7, S) = {(1, 1, 0)}, it follows that W3 = ⟨x⟩, W4 = ⟨y⟩, W5 = ⟨z⟩, W6 = ⟨x2⟩and W7 = ⟨xy⟩.

We finish this section with three lemmas that will be very useful in the sequel.
Lemma 3.11. Let r ∈S, r ̸= 0, r < La. Suppose that δr ≥2. Let η ∈N, 1 ≤η ≤ϕr,1.
(1) If {r + kq | k ∈[1, η]} ∩S = ∅, then [0, η] ⊆Gr.
(2) If {r + kq | k ∈[1, δr −1]} ∩S = ∅, then Vr = ker(ρGr
r ) = 0.
Proof. Note that 1 ≤δr −1 = κr = ϕr,1, so ϕr,1 ≥1 and [1, ϕr,1] is not empty. Suppose that there
exist k ∈[1, η] such that k is not r-good (recall that 0 ∈Gr). Thus, there exist s ∈S, s > r, and
j ∈[0, ϕs,1], such that r + kq = s + jq. Let k′ = k −j, so r + k′q = s ∈S. Since r < s, j ≥0 and
k ≤η, then 0 < k′ = k −j ≤k ≤η. So r + k′q ∈{r + lq | l ∈[1, η]} ∩S ̸= ∅. This proves (1).
Suppose that {r +kq | k ∈[1, δr −1]}∩S = ∅. Since 1 ≤δr −1 ≤ϕr,1, then, by (1), [0, δr −1] ⊆Gr.
Thus, card(Gr) ≥δr. By Corollary 3.9, Vr = ker(ρG
r ) = 0.
□
Lemma 3.12. Let r ∈S, r ̸= 0, r < La. Suppose that δr ≥2, that u = r + q ∈S and that
2 + ϕu,1 ≤ϕr,1. Let η ∈[2 + ϕu,1, ϕr,1]. If {r + kq | k ∈[2, η]} ∩S = ∅, then {0} ∪[2 + ϕu,1, η] ⊆Gr.
Proof. Recall that 0 ∈Gr. If there were k ∈[2+ϕu,1, η] such that k is not r-good, then there would
be s ∈S, s > r, and j ∈[0, ϕs,1], such that r + kq = s + jq. Hence r + k′q = s ∈S, for k′ := k −j,
where 1 ≤k′ = k −j ≤k ≤η. Since r + q ∈S and r + kq ̸∈S, for 2 ≤k ≤η, then, necessarily,
k′ = 1 and u = r + q = r + k′q = s. In particular, ϕu,1 = ϕs,1 and k ≥2 + ϕu,1 = 2 + ϕs,1. Since
j ≤ϕs,1, then 1 = k′ = k −j ≥2 + ϕs,1 −ϕs,1 = 2, a contradiction.
□
Lemma 3.13. Let r ∈S, r ̸= 0, r < La. If u = r+lq ∈S, for some l ≥1, then [l, l+ϕu,1]∩Gr = ∅.
Proof. This follows from Definition 3.6. Indeed, since Gr ⊆[0, ϕr,1], if l > ϕr,1, then l ̸∈Gr and
[l, l + ϕu,1] ∩Gr = ∅. If l ∈[1, ϕr,1], and since u = r + lq ∈S, then, by definition, l ̸∈Gr. Take
k ∈[l, l + ϕu,1] and write k = l + j, with j ∈[0, ϕu,1]. Let us see k ̸∈Gr. But this is clear, since
r + kq = r + lq + jq = u + jq, where u = r + lq ∈S, u > r and j ∈[0, ϕu,1].
□
4. Ensuring when the good kernels are zero
In this section we prove that if r < s0 = a(n −1) + 2, then Vr = ker(ρGr
r ) = 0. Note that
s0 = (n −2)a + (a + 2) ∈S (see Notation 2.1).
Example 4.1. Suppose that n = 3, so a = (n −1)n/2 = 3 and s0 = a(n −1) + 2 = 8. We have
seen in Example 3.10 that Vr = ker(ρGr
r ) = 0, for all r < s0 = 8.
Throughout, we intensively use Summary 2.5 and a version of [5, Theorem 3.1]. We include it
here for the sake of easy reference.
Proposition 4.2. Let n ≥3, a = (n−1)n/2 and S = ⟨a, a+1, a+2⟩. Let r ∈N and let r = aℓr+εr
be the Euclidean division in N of r by a, where ℓr = ⌊r/a⌋is the quotient and εr = r −aℓr is the
remainder. Let r = au + v, with u, v ∈N. The following hold.
(1) r ∈S if, and only if, εr ≤2ℓr.
(2) If 0 ≤v ≤2u, then r ∈S.
(3) If 0 ≤v ≤2u and r < La, then r ∈S and (u, v) = (ℓr, εr).
(4) If 2u < v < a, then (u, v) = (ℓr, εr) and r ̸∈S.
Proof. Item (1) is the equivalence (i) ⇔(ii) in [5, Theorem 3.1].
Item (2) follows from the
equivalence (i) ⇔(iv) in [5, Theorem 3.1]. Suppose that 0 ≤v ≤2u and r < La. Then, by [5,
Theorem 2.2], r ∈ULF(S), i.e., all the factorizations of r have the same length. By [5, Theorem 3.1,

then v < a and v = εr and u = ℓr. Thus, 2ℓr < εr, and by Item (1), one deduces that r ̸∈S.
□
Remark 4.3. If n ≥4 and r < s0, then r < La and one can apply [5, Theorems 3.1, 3.4].
Proof. If n = 4, then s0 = a(n −1) + 2 = 20 < ⌊a/2⌋(a + 2) = La = 24. Suppose that n ≥5.
By Summary 2.5, Frob(S) < La. Let us see that s0 ≤Frob(S). Indeed, if n = 2m + 1, with
m ≥2, then ⌊a/2⌋= ⌊(2m + 1)m/2⌋≥2m + 1 = n > n −1. If n = 2m + 2, with m ≥2,
then ⌊a/2⌋= (2m + 1)(m + 1) ≥2(m + 1) = n > n −1, as well. Therefore, ⌊a/2⌋> n −1, so
⌊a/2⌋−(n −1) ≥1. Thus, a(⌊a/2⌋−(n −1)) ≥a ≥3 = 2 + 1 and Frob(S) = a⌊a/2⌋−1 ≥s0.
□
Lemma 4.4. Let n ≥4. Let r ∈S, r ̸= 0, r < s0. Then ℓr ≤n −1. If in addition ℓr = n −1,
then δr = 1 and Vr = ker(ρGr
r ) = 0.

ℓr > n −1, then r = aℓr + εr ≥an + εr = a(n −1) + (a + εr) ≥a(n −1) + 3 > s0, a contradiction.
Suppose that ℓr = n −1. By [5, Theorem 3.4], n −1 = ℓr = 2(δr −1) + ιr, where δr ≥1 and ιr ≥0.
Thus, 0 ≤ιr ≤n −1. Moreover, a(n −1) + εr = aℓr + εr = r < s0, which forces εr ∈{0, 1}. Then,
cr = εr −ℓr = εr −n + 1 ∈{−n + 1, −n + 2}. Since n ≥4, then cr ≤−2 < 0. Recall that cr ∈Γιr,
where Γ0 = {0}, Γ1 = {−1, 0, 1} and, for ιr ≥2, Γιr = {−ιr, −ιr + 1, ιr −1, ιr}. Thus ιr ≥2 and

ιr = n −1. In particular, n −1 = ℓr = 2(δr −1) + ιr = 2(δr −1) + (n −1) and δr = 1. Suppose that

and δr = 1 again. Since δr = 1, then, by Corollary 3.9, Vr = ker(ρGr

Lemma 4.5. Let n ≥4. Let r ∈S, r ̸= 0, r < La. Suppose that δr ≥2. If 2ℓr < ℓr + c + kq < a,

Therefore, {r + kq | k ∈[1, δr −1]} ∩S = ∅. Thus, by Lemma 3.11, Vr = ker(ρG

Remark 4.6. Recall that q = 2⌊(n + 1)/2⌋−1. In particular, (q + 1)/2 = ⌊(n + 1)/2⌋. Note that,
if n is odd, then q = n and, if n is even, then q = n −1. In particular, ⌊n/2⌋q = a, q is always odd
and n −1 ≤q ≤n.

with ℓr = 4, δr = 2, ιr = 0 and cr = 0.

Remark 4.9. The idea of the proof of Theorem 4.7 is to find a large enough subset L of Gr, so
as to ensure that Vr = ker(ρL
r ) = 0. This is what we do in Cases 1, 2 and 3, where we show
[0, δr −1] ⊆Gr and then apply Lemma 3.11. In Case 4, we prove {r+kq | k ∈[2, η]}∩S = ∅, where
η = min(ϕr,1, ⌊n/2 −1⌋−1) ≥δr −1. In Subcases 4.1 and 4.1, we show 1 ∈Gr, so [0, η] ⊆Gr,
and since η ≥δr −1, we deduce Vr = ker(ρGr
r ) = 0. The most complicated case is Subcase 4.3, the
only one in which 1 ̸∈Gr. The reason of the election of η can be understood in the following way.
On the one hand, η must be smaller than or equal to ϕr,1, since Gr ⊂[0, ϕr,1]. On the other hand,
since ⌊n/2⌋q = a, then r + ⌊n/2⌋q = r + a ∈S and ⌊n/2⌋̸∈Gr. Taking η = min(ϕr,1, ⌊n/2⌋−1),
we are able to show that {0} ∪[2 + ϕu,1, η] ⊆Gr, which is enough to our purposes.
5. Bases for the good kernels
In this section we give a basis of ker(ρGr
r ), for r ∈[s0, s0 +n), where s0 = a(n−1)+2 and n ≥6.
Remark 5.1. Note that, if n ≥5, then n(n −5) ≥0, so n(n −1)/4 ≥n and ⌊a/2⌋≥n ≥1.
Thus, a(⌊a/2⌋−n) + 2(⌊a/2⌋−1) ≥0 and ⌊a/2⌋(a + 2) ≥an + 2 = s0 + a > s0 + n. Therefore,
s0 + n < ⌊a/2⌋(a + 2) ≤La.
Though all the results in this section hold for n = 5, to facilitate the reading and to avoid
distinguishing the limit case n = 5, we suppose that n ≥6. We also suppose r < s0 + n. In
particular, r < La, and one can apply [5, Notations 2.9 and Theorems 3.1 and 3.4] (see also
Proposition 4.2). Therefore, given r ∈S, r ̸= 0, r < s0 + n, we can write
r = aℓr + εr = (a + 1)(2δr −2 + ιr) + cr, with
ℓr = ⌊r/a⌋, 0 ≤εr < a, εr ≤2ℓr, δr ≥1, ℓr = 2δr −2 + ιr, 0 ≤ιr ≤ℓr, cr ∈Γιr and cr = εr −ℓr.
Lemma 5.2. Let n ≥6. If r ∈[s0, s0 + n), then ℓr = n −1. Moreover, [s0, s0 + n) ⊂S.
Proof. Let r ∈[s0, s0 + n). If ℓr ≥n, then an + εr ≤aℓr + εr = r < s0 + n = a(n −1) + 2 + n and
a+εr < n+2. Since n ≥6, then (n−1)/2 ≥2. Thus, 2n ≤(n−1)n/2 = a ≤a+εr < n+2 and n < 2,
a contradiction. If ℓ≤n−2, then a(n−1)+2 = s0 ≤r = aℓr+εr ≤a(n−2)+εr and a+2 ≤εr < a,
a contradiction. Thus, ℓr = n −1 and r = a(n −1) + εr. If r ≤s0 + n −1 = a(n −1) + n + 1, then
εr ≤n + 1 ≤2(n −1) = 2ℓr. By Proposition 4.2, (1), r ∈S.
□

19
As before, we distinguish the pairs (ιr, cr) by using the same colors as in [5].
Lemma 5.3. Let n ≥6. Let r ∈[s0, s0 + n). Write r = (a + 1)(2δr −2 + ιr) + cr. The following
hold.
(1) ιr is odd if and only if n is even, i.e., ιr ≡n −1 (mod 2).
(2) If cr ≥0, then ιr ≤3 and the pair (ιr, cr) is in {(0, 0), (1, 0), (1, 1), (2, 1), (2, 2), (3, 2)}.
(3) If cr < 0, then ιr ≤n −3 and the pair (ιr, cr) is in
{(1, −1)} ∪{(i, −i), (i, −i + 1) | 2 ≤i ≤n −3, i ≡n −1 (mod 2)}.
(4) δr ≥2.
Proof. Since 2δr −2 + ιr = ℓr = (n −1), then ιr ≡ℓr = n −1 (mod 2). Recall that cr ∈Γιr, where
Γ0 = {0}, Γ1 = {−1, 0, 1} and Γi = {−i, −i + 1, i −1, i}, for i ≥2. Substituting the expression
r = (a+1)ℓr+cr = a(n−1)+n−1+cr in s0 ≤r < s0+n, we get a(n−1)+2 ≤a(n−1)+n−1+cr <
a(n −1) + 2 + n, so 2 ≤n −1 + cr < 2 + n. Therefore, 3 −n ≤cr < 3.
Suppose that cr ≥0. Then, ιr −1 ≤cr < 3 and ιr ≤3. Thus, 0 ≤ιr ≤3 and 0 ≤cr ≤2 and
the only possible pairs (ιr, cr) are (0, 0), (1, 0), (1, 1), (2, 1), (2, 2) and (3, 2).
Suppose that cr < 0. In particular, ιr > 0. Then, 3 −n ≤cr ≤−ιr + 1 and 0 < ιr ≤n −2.
Since n and ιr have distinct parity, ιr ≤n −3. Thus, if ιr = 1, then cr = −1 and (ιr, cr) = (1, −1).
If ιr ≥2, then cr = −ιr or cr = −ιr + 1, where 2 ≤ιr ≤n −3 and ιr ≡n −1 (mod 2).
Since ℓr = n−1 and ℓr = 2δr −2+ιr, then δr = (n+1−ιr)/2. If cr ≥0, by (2) and since n ≥6,
ιr ≤3 ≤n −3; if cr < 0, by (3), ιr ≤n −3. Thus, δr = (n + 1 −ιr)/2 ≥(n + 1 −n + 3)/2 = 2.
□
Next result stratifies the elements of [s0, s0 + n) into five subsets, when n is odd, and into six
subsets, when n is even. This will be crucial in the sequel.
Proposition 5.4. Let n ≥6. Let r ∈[s0, s0 + n). Write r = rk = s0 + k −1, where 1 ≤k ≤n.
Suppose that n is odd, n ≥7.
• If 1 ≤k ≤n −4, k odd, then crk = −ιrk, 2 ≤ιrk ≤n −3, ιrk ≡n −1 (mod 2). In fact,
ιrk = n −k −2.
• If 2 ≤k ≤n −3, k even, then crk = −ιrk + 1, 2 ≤ιrk ≤n −3, ιrk ≡n −1 (mod 2). In
fact, ιrk = n −k −1.
• (ιrn−2, crn−2) = (0, 0), (ιrn−1, crn−1) = (2, 1) (ιrn, crn) = (2, 2).
Suppose that n is even, n ≥6.
• If 1 ≤k ≤n −5, k odd, then crk = −ιrk, 3 ≤ιrk ≤n −3, ιrk ≡n −1 (mod 2). In fact,
ιrk = n −k −2.
• If 2 ≤k ≤n −4, k even, then crk = −ιrk + 1, 3 ≤ιrk ≤n −3, ιrk ≡n −1 (mod 2). In
fact, ιrk = n −k −1.
• (ιrn−3, crn−3) = (1, −1), (ιrn−2, crn−2) = (1, 0), (ιrn−1, crn−1) = (1, 1), (ιrn, crn) = (3, 2).
Proof. By Lemma 5.2, ℓr = n −1 and by Lemma 5.3, ιrk ≡n −1 (mod 2).
Moreover, rk =
s0 + k −1 = a(n −1) + k + 1 and rk = aℓrk + εrk = a(n −1) + εrk. Thus, εrk = k + 1.

Next, we give a sufficient condition to ensure that an element of the form s = r + kq satisfies
s < La. This is relevant in order to apply [5, Theorems 3.1 and 3.4].

Lemma 5.5. Let n ≥6. Let r ∈[s0, s0 + n), and let s = r + kq, for some k ≥0.
(1) Then ⌊a/2⌋−n ≥1 and, if n ≥7, then ⌊a/2⌋−n ≥3.
(2) If k ≤(⌊a/2⌋−n)⌊n/2⌋, then s < Frob(S) < La.
(3) If k ≤⌊n/2⌋, then s < Frob(S) < La.
(4) If k = (⌊a/2⌋−n + 1)⌊n/2⌋, then Frob(S) < s < ⌊a/2⌋(a + 2) ≤La.
Proof. Observe that, since n ≥6, then n2 −5n−4 ≥0, so n(n−1) ≥4n+4, n(n−1)/4 ≥n+1 and
⌊a/2⌋−n ≥1. Similarly, if n ≥7, then n2 −5n −12 ≥0, n(n −1) ≥4n + 12, n(n −1)/4 ≥n + 3
and ⌊a/2⌋−n ≥3. Suppose that k ≤(⌊a/2⌋−n)⌊n/2⌋. Since ⌊n/2⌋q = a, then s = r + kq ≤
s0 + n −1 + kq ≤a(n −1) + n + 1 + (⌊a/2⌋−n)a = ⌊a/2⌋a −a + n −1. Since n ≥6, then a > n
and s = r + kq < a⌊a/2⌋−1 = Frob(S).

Proof. By Lemma 5.2, ℓr = n−1 = 2d−2+i and, by Lemma 5.3, d ≥2 and n±i is always odd. In
particular, d = (n+1−i)/2 and εr = ℓ+c = n−1−i is even. Therefore, ϕr,1 = ℓr −⌊(εr +1)/2⌋=
n −1 −⌊(n −i)/2⌋= (n −1 + i)/2, ϕr,2 = εr −2⌊εr/2⌋= 0 and ϕr,3 = ⌊εr/2⌋= (n −1 −i)/2. Since
ℓ= n−1 = 2d−2+i, then ϕr,1 = (n−1+i)/2 = (2d−2+i+i)/2 = i+d−1 and ϕr,1 −(d−1) = i.
Thus, Ir = [ϕr,1 −(d −1), ϕr,1] = [i, (n −1 + i)/2].
Note that since i ≥2, then ϕr,1 = (n −1 + i)/2 ≥⌊n/2⌋.
Let us see that s1 = r + q ∈S. Since s1 = r + q = (a + 1)ℓ+ c + q = a(n −1) + n −1 −i + q,
by Proposition 4.2, it is enough to see that 0 ≤(n −1 −i + q) ≤2(n −1). Since q ≤n and
i ≥2, then n −1 −i + q ≤n −1 −2 + n < 2(n −1). Since q ≥n −1 and i ≤n −3, then
n −1 −i + q ≥n −1 −n + 3 + n −1 = n + 1 ≥0. Note that s1 = r + q < La, because 1 ≤⌊n/2⌋
and, by Lemma 5.5, s1 = r + 1 · q < Frob(S) < La.

If n is odd, then i is even, q = n and (n−2+i−q)/2 = (i−2)/2 = ⌊(i−1)/2⌋. If n is even, then i is
odd, q = n−1 and (n−2+i−q)/2 = (i−1)/2 = ⌊(i−1)/2⌋as well. Therefore, ϕs1,1 = ⌊(i−1)/2⌋.
Moreover, ϕs1,2 = εs1 −2⌊εs1/2⌋= 1 and ϕs1,3 = ⌊εs1/2⌋= n −⌊(i + 3)/2⌋. This shows (1).
Note that 2 + ϕs1,1 = 2 + ⌊(i −1)/2⌋≤n/2 + ⌊(i −1)/2⌋≤(n −1 + i)/2 = ϕr,1.

Since ℓ= n −1 = 2d −2 + i, then ϕr,1 = (n −3 + i)/2 = i + d −2 and ϕr,1 −(d −1) = i −1. Thus,
Ir = [ϕr,1 −(d −1), ϕr,1] = [i −1, (n −3 + i)/2].
Observe that, since i ≥2, then ϕr,1 = (n −3 + i)/2 ≥⌊n/2⌋. Indeed, if i = 2, then n i odd and
(n −3 + i)/2 = (n −1)/2 = ⌊n/2⌋; if i ≥3, then (n −3 + i)/2 ≥n/2 ≥⌊n/2⌋as well.
Let us see that s1 = r + q ∈S. Since s1 = r + q = (a + 1)ℓ+ c + q = a(n −1) + n −i + q, by

n−i+q ≤n−2+n = 2(n−1). Since q ≥n−1 and i ≤n−3, then n−i+q ≥n−n+3+n−1 = n+2 ≥0.
Note that s1 < La, because 1 ≤⌊n/2⌋and, by Lemma 5.5, s1 = r + 1 · q < Frob(S) < La.

n −i + q is even. Therefore, ϕs1,1 = ℓs1 −⌊(εs1 + 1)/2⌋= n −1 −⌊(n −i + q)/2⌋= (n −2 + i −q)/2.
If n is odd, then i is even, q = n and (n−2+i−q)/2 = (i−2)/2 = ⌊(i−1)/2⌋. If n is even, then i is
odd, q = n−1 and (n−2+i−q)/2 = (i−1)/2 = ⌊(i−1)/2⌋as well. Therefore, ϕs1,1 = ⌊(i−1)/2⌋.
Moreover, ϕs1,2 = εs1 −2⌊εs1/2⌋= 0 and ϕs1,3 = ⌊εs1/2⌋= n −⌊(i + 1)/2⌋. This shows (1).
Since n ≥6, then 2 ≤(n−2)/2 and 2+ϕs1,1 = 2+(n−2+i−q)/2 ≤(n−2)/2+(n−2+i−q)/2 =
(n −3 + i)/2 = ϕr,1.
Since s1 = r + q ∈S, then, by Lemma 3.13, [1, 1 + ϕs1,1] ∩Gr = [1, 1 + ⌊(i −1)/2⌋] ∩Gr = ∅.

By Remark 4.6, ⌊n/2⌋q = a, so s2 = r + a ∈S.

Lemma 5.14. Let n ≥6. Let r ∈[s0, s0 + n). Write r = rk = s0 + k −1, with 1 ≤k ≤n. Suppose
that (ιr, cr) = (3, 2). Then n is even, k = n, r = rn and Ir = Gr = [0, (n −4)/2]. Moreover,
Vr = ker(ρGr
r ) = 0.
Proof. By Proposition 5.4, if (ιrk, crk) = (3, 2), then n is even, k = n and r = rn. By Lemma 5.2,
ℓr = n −1 = 2d −2 + i = 2d + 1. In particular, d = (n −2)/2 and εr = ℓ+ c = n + 1 is odd.
Therefore, ϕr,1 = ℓr −⌊(εr + 1)/2⌋= n −1 −⌊(n + 2)/2⌋= (n −4)/2, ϕr,2 = εr −2⌊εr/2⌋= 0 and
ϕr,3 = ⌊εr/2⌋= n/2. Moreover, since d = (n−2)/2, then ϕr,1 = (n−4)/2 = d−1, ϕr,1 −(d−1) = 0
and Ir = [ϕr,1 −(d −1), ϕr,1] = [0, (n −4)/2]. Moreover, Gr ⊆[0, ϕr,1] = [0, (n −4)/2].
Let us prove that r + kq ̸∈S, for all k ∈[1, d −1]. Note that d −1 = (n −4)/2 < ⌊n/2⌋. Since
n ≥6 and k ≤d −1 ≤⌊n/2⌋, by Lemma 5.5, r + kq < Frob(S), for all k ∈[0, d −1]. We have
r + kq = (a + 1)ℓ+ c + kq = aℓ+ (ℓ+ 2 + kq). Since ℓ= n −1, k ≤d −1, q = n −1, d = (n/2) −1
and dq = a −(n −1), then ℓ+ 2 + kq ≤n + 1 + (d −1)q = n + 1 + a −2(n −1) = a −n + 3 < a.
Since ℓ= n −1, k ≥1, q = n −1, then ℓ+ 2 + kq ≥2n > 2ℓ. Thus, 2ℓ≤(ℓ+ 2 + kq) < a. By
Proposition 4.2, r + kq ̸∈S.
Thus, {r + kq | k ∈[1, d −1]} T S = ∅. By Lemma 3.11, [0, d −1] ⊆Gr. So, Gr = [0, d −1].
Since d = (n −2)/2, then d −1 = (n −4)/2 and Gr = [0, (n −4)/2]. Moreover, card(Gr) = d. By
Corollary 3.9, Vr = ker(ρGr
r ) = 0.
□

6. Finding a part of a minimal generating set of the kernel
In this section we show that Vr = ker(ρGr
r ), for all r ∈[s0, s0 + n), n ≥6. As a consequence, and
using the Extending to basis Method, we deduce a part of a minimal generating set of ker(ρ).
The proof consists in finding elements f ∈ker(ρ) such that fσ is (part of) a basis of ker(ρGr
r ).
These elements can be obtained explicitly on solving at most four n × n linear systems. We divide
the reasoning into several lemmas according to which subset of the partition of [s0, s0 + n) given
in Proposition 5.4 contains the element r. As always, for the sake of readability, we write in the
proofs ℓ, d, i and c instead of ℓr, δr, ιr and cr, if no confusion is possible.
Next observation follows directly from Lemma 3.1, (1), Lemma 3.2, (1), and Notation 2.2. We
state it here for easy quotation and since it will be used intensively all along the section.

Proof. Observe that r = (a + 1)ℓr + cr = (a + 1)(n −1) −i. Clearly, r < s1 < s2 < s3 < s4. Let us
prove that s4 < La. If n = 6, then a = 15, q = n−1 = 5, i is odd and, since 2 ≤i ≤n−3, it follows
that i = 3 and r = 77. Thus, s4 = r + (2⌊n/2⌋+ 1)q = 112 and La = (⌊a/2⌋+ 2)a = 135. Suppose

Now, we can state and prove the main result of the section.
Theorem 6.11. Let n ≥3. The set of polynomials {f1, . . . , fn} is part of a minimal generating
set of ker(ρ).
Proof. The previous Summary 6.10 shows the cases n ≥6. It remains to prove the cases n = 3, 4, 5.
Suppose that n = 3. Then a = (n −1)n/2 = 3, s0 = a(n −1) + 2 = 8 and S = ⟨3, 4, 5⟩. By
Theorem 4.7, Vr = 0, for all r ∈S ∩(0, 8). Let us calculate Vr, for r ∈[s0, s0 + n) = {8, 9, 10}.
Since F(8, S) = {(1, 0, 1), (0, 2, 0)}, F(9, S) = {(3, 0, 0), (0, 1, 1)} and F(10, S) = {(2, 1, 0), (0, 0, 2)},
then W8 = ⟨xz, y2⟩, W9 = ⟨x3, yz⟩and W10 = ⟨x2y, z2⟩. By Lemma 3.1, V8 = 0 or V8 = ⟨xz −y2⟩,
V9 = 0 or V9 = ⟨x3 −yz⟩and V10 = 0 or V10 = ⟨x2y −z2⟩. For k = 1, 2, 3, let gk and fk be as
in (2.1). Note that g1 = xz −y2 ∈W8 and f1 = g1 −xy2 + yz2 satisfies ρ(f1) = 0 and fσ
1 = g1.
Similarly, g2 = x3 −yz ∈W9 and f2 = g2 −3xyz + z3 −x2yz + xz3 satisfies ρ(f2) = 0 and fσ
2 = g2.
Finally, g3 = x2y −z2 ∈W10 and f3 = g3 −y2z −xz2 satisfies ρ(f3) = 0 and fσ
3 = g3. Therefore,

7. Proof of the main result: a minimal generating set of the kernel
Let f1, . . . , fn be as in Definition 2.3, where, for n ≥6, we take the coefficients given by Lem-
mas 6.2, 6.3, 6.4, 6.5, 6.6, 6.7, 6.8, 6.9. The purpose of this section is to prove that {f1, . . . , fn}
defines a minimal generating set of ker(ρ). We start with some previous results.
Lemma 7.1. Let n ≥6. Let R = k[[x, y, z]], m = (x, y, z) and I = (f1, . . . , fn).
(1) If n is odd and 1 ≤k ≤n −3, then (fk, z) = (xn−k−2yk+1, z).
(2) If n is even and 2 ≤k ≤n −3, then (fk, z) = (xn−k−2yk+1, z).
(3) (fn−2, z) = (yn−1, z).
(4) If n is even, then (f1, fn−2, z) = (xn−3y2, yn−1, z).
(5) (fn−2, fn−1, z) = (xn, yn−1, z).
(6) (fn−2, fn, z) = (xn−1y, yn−1, z).
(7) I + zR = (xn, xn−1y, xn−3y2, . . . , xyn−2, yn−1, z).

Lemma 7.2. Let n ≥3. Let R = k[[x, y, z]], m = (x, y, z) and I = (f1, . . . , fn). Then ht(I) = 2,
I + zR is an m-primary ideal of R and lengthR(R/(I + zR)) = a + 2.
Proof. Let P = ker(ρ). By Theorem 6.11, I = (f1, . . . , fn) ⊆P. Clearly, P ⊂m, because if g ̸∈m,
then g is invertible, 1 = ρ(gg−1) = ρ(g)ρ(g−1) and g ̸∈P. Since ρ(z) = ta+2, z ̸∈P. Thus,
I ⊆P ⊊m. Since P is prime, then ht(I) ≤ht(P) < ht(m) = 3. Therefore, ht(I) ≤ht(P) ≤2.
Suppose that n = 3. Let f1, f2, f3 be as in (2.1). Then (f1, f2, z) = (x3, (1+x)y2, z) = (x3, y2, z).
By [3, Corollary 1.6.19.], grade(f1, f2, z) = grade(x3, y2, z) = 3 and f1, f2, z is a regular sequence.
Therefore, 2 ≤grade(I) = ht(I) ≤ht(P) ≤2 and ht(I) = ht(P) = 2. Since I+zR = (x3, x2y, y2, z),

Lemma 7.3. Let n ≥3. Let R = k[[x, y, z]], m = (x, y, z), P = ker(ρ) and D = R/P. The
following hold.
(1) The ideal P has height 2 and the length of R/(P + zR) is finite as an R-module.
(2) D = k[[ta + ta+q, ta+1, ta+2]] is a one-dimensional Cohen-Macaulay local domain.
(3) D[t] = k[[t]].
(4) The field of fractions of D is K(D) = K(k[[t]]).
(5) The integral closure of D = k[[ta + ta+q, ta+1, ta+2]] in its field of fractions is k[[t]].
(6) If V = k[[t]], then lengthR(R/(P + zR)) = lengthD(D/zD) = lengthD(V/zV ).
(7) lengthR(R/(P + zR)) = lengthD(V/zV ) = lengthV (V/zV ) = a + 2.

Proof of (1). By Theorem 6.11, I = (f1, . . . , fn) ⊆P, where P ⊊m, and I + zR ⊆P + zR ⊂m.
By Lemma 7.2, ht(I) = 2 and I + zR is m-primary. Thus, ht(P) = 2, P + zR is m-primary,
ht(P + zR) = 3, R/(P + zR) is Artinian and lengthR(R/(P + zR)) is finite.
• Proof of (2). Observe that R is a regular local ring with maximal ideal m, in particular, Cohen-
Macaulay local.
Thus, dim(D) = dim(R/P) = dim(R) −ht(P) = 1.
Since R is Noetherian
local and P is a prime ideal of R, then D = R/P is a Noetherian local domain. In particular,
any non-zero and non-invertible element of D is a D-regular sequence, so 1 ≤grade(mD, D) =
depth(D) ≤dim(D) = 1 and D is a Cohen-Macaulay ring. Let mD = m/P be its maximal ideal
and kD = D/mD = R/m = k its residue field. By abuse of notation, let x, y, z still denote the
classes of the variables in D, so mD = (x, y, z). Note that D = R/P = k[[x, y, z]]/ ker(ρ) ∼= Im(ρ) =
k[[ta + ta+q, ta+1, ta+2]]. Through this isomorphism, x, y and z are identified with ta + ta+q, ta+1
and ta+2, respectively.
• Proof of (3). Clearly, D[t] = k[[ta + ta+q, ta+1, ta+2]][t] is a subring of k[[t]]. Let us prove the
equality. To do that, consider the numerical semigroup S = ⟨a+1, a+2⟩, whose Frobenius number
is Frob(S) = (a + 1)(a + 2) −(a + 1) −(a + 2) = a2 + a −1 (see, e.g., [18, Theorem 2.1.1]).
Let f ∈k[[t]]. Write f = φ1 + φ2, where φ1 := Pa2+a−1

i≥0 λa2+a+ita2+a+i.
Clearly φ1 ∈k[t] ⊂D[t]. Since a2 + a −1 is the last number not in S, it follows that ta2+a+i ∈
k[[ta+1, ta+2]] for all i ≥0. Thus, φ2 ∈D. So, f ∈D[t] and D[t] = k[[t]].
• Proof of (4). Since D = k[[ta + ta+q, ta+1, ta+2]] ⊂k[[t]], then K(D) ⊆K(k[[t]]). To see the
other inclusion, take g ∈K(k[[t]]). By Step 5, k[[t]] = D[t]. Thus, g ∈K(k[[t]]) = K(D[t]) and
g = p(t)/q(t), where p(t), q(t) ∈D[t], q(t) ̸= 0. Since ta+1 and ta+2 are in D, then t = ta+2/ta+1 ∈
K(D). Since K(D) is a field that contains t and D, it follows that K(D) contains too any quotient
of polynomial expressions in t with coefficients in D, thus g = p(t)/q(t) ∈K(D).
• Proof of (5). The element t is a root of the monic polynomial T a+1 −ta+1 ∈D[T]. Thus, t is
integral over D and D ⊂D[t] is an integral extension. Since k[[t]] is regular local, it is integrally
closed in its field of fractions. Since D ⊂D[t] = k[[t]] is an integral extension, k[[t]] is integrally
closed in K((k[[t]]) and K(D) = K(k[[t]]), then the integral closure of D is k[[t]].
• Proof of (6). Since R →D = R/P is a surjective map and D/zD = R/(P + zR) is a D-module,
it follows that lengthR(R/(P + zR)) = lengthR(D/zD) = lengthD(D/zD).
Since z is a D-regular sequence in mD and D is a one dimensional Cohen-Macaulay local ring,
then z is a system of parameters of D (see, e.g., [12, §14] and [3, Theorem 2.1.2]).
Let V = k[[t]], which is a discrete valuation ring, DVR for short. Set mV = (t) its maximal ideal
and kV = V/mV = k its residue field.
We have seen that D ⊂D[t] = k[[t]] = V , where t is integral over D. Thus, V is a finitely
generated D-module (see, e.g., [2, Proposition 5.1]). Since ta+2 is a V -regular sequence in mD,
then 1 ≤grade(mD, V ) = depthD(V ) ≤dim V = 1 and V is a finitely generated Cohen-Macaulay
D-module (see, e.g., [3, Definition 1.2.7, Proposition 1.2.12 and Definition 2.1.1]).
Note that K(D) = (D \ {0})−1D ⊆(D \ {0})−1V ⊆(D \ {0})−1K(D) = K(D). Therefore,
V ⊗D K(D) = (D \ {0})−1V = K(D). So, rankD(V ) = 1 (see, e.g., [3, Definition 1.4.2]). Using
that D is a Cohen-Macaulay local ring, that V is a finitely generated Cohen-Macaulay D-module
of rankD(V ) = 1 and that z is a system of parameters of D, we conclude that lengthD(V/zV ) =
lengthD(D/zD) · rankD(V ) = lengthD(D/zD) (see, e.g., [3, Corollary 4.6.11]).

We can prove now the main result of the paper.
Theorem 7.4. Let n ≥3. Then {f1, . . . , fn} defines a minimal generating set of ker(ρ).
Proof. As in the previous lemmas, set I = (f1, . . . , fn) and P = ker(ρ). Since ρ(z) = ta+2, then
z ̸∈P, P ∩zR = zP and Tor1(R/P, R/zR) = 0. Consider the short exact sequence
0 →P/I →R/I →R/P →0.
On tensor this short exact sequence by R/zR we obtain:
· · · →Tor1(R/P, R/zR)
|
{z
}
=0
→(P/I)/z(P/I) →R/(I + zR) →R/(P + zR) →0.
By Lemmas 7.2 and 7.3, lengthR(R/(I +zR)) = lengthR(R/(P +zR)) = a+2. By the additivity of
the length, we get lengthR((P/I)/z(P/I)) = 0 and P/I = z(P/I). By Nakayama’s Lemma, I = P.
By Theorem 6.11, f1, . . . , fn is part of a minimal generating set of P, so µ(P) ≥n. Since I = P,
then f1, . . . , fn is a generating set of P, so µ(P) ≤n. Hence, µ(P) = n and f1, . . . , fn is a minimal
system of generators of P.
□

Corollary 7.5. Let k a field of characteristic zero. Consider the following commutative diagram
of k-algebra homomorphisms.

where ϱ1(x) = ϱ2(x) = ρ(x) = ta(1+tq), ϱ1(y) = ϱ2(y) = ρ(y) = ta+1, ϱ1(z) = ϱ2(z) = ρ(z) = ta+2,
φ1 and ψ1 are the localization homomorphisms and φ2 and ψ2 are the completion homomorphisms.
Then the prime ideals ker(ϱ1) and ker(ϱ2) are minimally generated by at least n elements.
Proof. Let H2 = (f1, . . . , fn)A2 be the ideal of A2 generated by the polynomials f1, . . . , fn. By
Theorem 7.4, H2R = ⟨φ2(H2)⟩= (f1, . . . , fn)R is equal to ker(ρ), which is a prime ideal of R
minimally generated by f1, . . . , fn. Since the completion homomorphism φ2 is faithfully flat, H2 =
H2R ∩A2, i.e., contraction is left inverse to extension of ideals (see, e.g., [12, §8, page 63]). In
particular, H2 is a prime ideal of A2. Moreover, any generating set of H2 has at least n elements,
otherwise, if H2 = (g1, . . . , gm), with m < n, then ker(ρ) = H2R = (g1, . . . , gm)R would be
generated by less than n elements, a contradiction. Since ρ ◦φ2 = ψ2 ◦ϱ2 and ψ2 is injective, it
follows that ψ2(ϱ2(fi)) = ρ(φ2(fi)) = ρ(fi) = 0 and fi ∈ker(ϱ2). Thus, H2 ⊆ker(ϱ2). Since H2
is a non-principal prime ideal of a Unique Factorization Domain, ht(H2) > 1. So H2 ⊆ker(ϱ2)
are prime ideals with 1 < ht(H2) ≤ht(ker(ϱ2)) < 3. Therefore, ht(H2) = ht(ker(ϱ2)) = 2 and
H2 = ker(ϱ2).

In connection with the conjecture of Sally in [19, Remark p. 53], we have:
Corollary 7.6. Let (A, m) be a 3-dimensional regular local ring containing a field k of characteristic
zero. Let n ∈N, n ≥3. Then there exists a prime ideal p of A minimally generated by n elements.
Proof. Let (R, n) = ( ˆA, ˆm) be the m-adic completion of (A, m). If m = (x, y, z), then n = (x, y, z)R.
Since A is regular local and A ⊃k, then R is a complete regular local ring containing the field k.
Hence, R is isomorphic to the power series ring k[[x, y, z]] in the variables x, y, z over the field k.
Let f1, . . . , fn ∈R be defined as in Definition 2.3 and P = (f1, . . . , fn)R. By Theorem 7.4, P is a
prime ideal of R minimally generated by f1, . . . , fn. Since f1, . . . , fn are polynomials in x, y, z with
coefficients in k, they can also be considered as elements of A. Let p = (f1, . . . , fn)A be the ideal
they generate in A. By definition, pR = P. Since the completion homomorphism φ : A →ˆA = R
is faithfully flat, it follows that p = pR ∩A. Thus p is prime. Moreover, f1, . . . , fn is a minimal
generating set of p for, if p = (q1, . . . , qm)A, with m < n, then P = pR = (q1, . . . , qm)R would be
generated by m elements, a contradiction.
□
8. Example and a Python code
To help understanding the whole process of the proof, we detail a little bit the case n = 6.
Example 8.1. Let n = 6. Then a = (n −1)n/2 = 15, S = ⟨15, 16, 17⟩, q = 2⌊(n + 1)/2⌋−1 = 5,
and ρ(x) = t15(1 + t5), ρ(y) = t16, ρ(z) = t17. Moreover, s0 = a(n −1) + 2 = 77.
According to Summary 6.10, for n = 6, the σ-leading forms g1, . . . , g6 of the minimal generating
set f1, . . . , f6 are determined by the subspaces Vr, where r ∈[s0, s0 + n −1) = {77, 78, 79, 80, 81}.

Remark 8.2. We have developed a Python code that computes the minimal generating set
f1, . . . , fn of ker(ρ). It is a pleasure to thank Oriol Almirall for his help to the development of
this code.
We briefly explain the structure of the code.
First, we define the function build_pascal(n), which constructs the n × n lower-triangular
Pascal matrix. This matrix will be used to compute the binomial determinants in a more efficient
way.
Next, we create the function bin_det(I,J) that computes the binomial matrix BI
J and its
determinant bI
J, when the sets I and J (defined as Python lists) have the same length.
By
default, this function uses the Pascal matrix defined previously so to compute more efficiently.
However, the same results can be obtained from the SymPy command sy.binomial by calling
bin_det(I,J,False).
Finally, the function get_minimal_gen(n) computes the minimal generating set f1, . . . , fn for
the ideal ker(ρ), when n ≥5. Note that the equalities in Definition 2.3 for the specific case n = 5
and for the general case n odd, n ≥7, coincide if we allow in the general case n to be equal to 5.
The acquisition of f1, . . . , fn proceeds in two main phases.
First, we compute the σ-leading forms g1, . . . , gn. They are obtained directly by applying Defini-
tion 2.3. Here, we need to calculate some binomial determinants. We do so by using the command
bin_det(I,J)[2] (calling the bin_det function with the second argument obtain just the deter-
minant).
Second, we define the tails.
Here we solve the linear systems described in Section 6.
Each
system is expressed in terms of binomial matrices, which again are computed by using the bin_det
function (in this case, called with the first argument). The systems are solved either using the
solve command or alternatively the gauss_jordan_solve command from the SymPy library.
As said before, the main advantage of our method is that we do not have to calculate any Gr¨obner
basis. Computing a minimal generating set for n ≥60 using a Gr¨obner basis approach often leads
to timeout, whereas our Python algorithm can still run efficiently.
For the implementation details and the full code, we refer the reader to the GitHub repository:
https://github.com/laura-gonzalez-hernandez/minimal-generators
